We do not know what he knows, or what evidence he may have hidden somewhere. We are sitting on a ticking time bomb. One day it will explode, and then it will be too late.”

Evan tried to interrupt, and this time Benedikt did not let him. “I have a plan. On the pretext of letting him go, I will follow him. If he turns, I will have him killed. If not, I will do as you suggest. The exposure we have with Daniel cannot be taken lightly.”

There was a pause on the line. Whatever Evan thought of it, he did not argue. In a sceptical tone Evan said, “You have created a lot of unrest. Make sure you handle this one with perfection.” The line went dead.

Sanchez had heard every word. Before Benedikt could put forward any explanation, she said, “You don’t have to explain. I know why you said what you said.” She wore a pensive smile, and she turned her face away from him to the window.

* * *

*Rudnya, 9:45 PM*

Sanchez waited until it was nearly dark before she crossed the landing to Benedikt’s room.

They had spent the afternoon in Rudnya, the first town on the Russian side of the road, a place of wooden houses with carved window frames, a railway station and a small church with a dome. The guesthouse stood on the edge of the town behind a timber fence. The woman at the desk had asked for one passport, not two, and Benedikt had paid her in roubles for two rooms and she had asked nothing more.

Benedikt opened the door before she knocked twice. The room was small and plain, with a narrow bed, a table by the window and a lamp with a fringed shade. The Makarov lay on the table beside his car keys. He moved it to the windowsill and pulled out a chair for her, and he sat down on the other side of the table and waited.

“In Vitebsk you said you had told the Russians to get rid of him,” Sanchez said. “I want to know exactly what you said. The words. And I want to know why.”

He did not look away. “It was October, on my first tour as head of the route. I stopped with Kozlov’s man at a café on the ring road outside Vitebsk, where the lorries stop. A man was carrying sacks of potatoes into the kitchen. I knew his face. He knew mine. I turned to the Russian and I said, ‘That man ran from the DAZZLE. He should not be anywhere near my route. Get rid of him.’ Those were the words.”

“And what did you mean by them?”

“I told myself I meant, take him off my route. I did not ask what they would do. I did not ask because I did not want to know. That night a car with Smolensk plates took him from the yard behind the café. In my report to Ayr I wrote that a former route worker had been found at Vitebsk and handed to the partners. Evan read it the way I knew he would read it. I let him.”

“Why?” It came out harder than she intended. “Why would one man unloading potatoes frighten the head of security?”

“Because he knew my face,” Benedikt said. “Because I had just been given the route, and he was the one man in Belarus who could say where he had seen me before.”

Sanchez held very still. She had known this was coming since Vitebsk, since before Vitebsk, and still she was not ready. “You said you were there on the day my father was captured. Which day, Benedikt?”

For a long time he said nothing. Outside a dog barked somewhere in the town and stopped.

“Girbin,” he said. “The fourteenth of February, 2010. When you said the bakery, in Tallinn, I knew. I did not want to know, so I asked you for a photograph, and then I knew.” He spoke slowly, like a man reading out a statement. “I had been with TransPacific three weeks. My chief was a Dane called Carsten Mørch. He took me with him because he said I should see how things were done. There was Mørch, a driver, one of Kozlov’s Russians and me. We waited in a black Range Rover by the square, facing the bakery, with the engine running. When your father came out with his bag they took him by the arms and put him in the car beside me. I sat in the back seat. I did not touch him. I did nothing. In the car he told them that if he did not come home, his pictures would go to the police and the papers. They took the camera from his van and found it empty, and they believed him. That is why he is alive. It was not because of anything I did.”

“Did you know his name?”

“Mørch called him Daniel. It was the only name I had, for four years. I never knew he had a surname until you put his face on my desk.”

“I was at my window,” Sanchez said. “I was sixteen. I saw the car.”

“I did not look at the windows.” He said it without asking for anything. “I have never told this to anyone. Not Damian. Not a priest. Nobody.”

She looked at his hands. At dinner in Tallinn and on the deck at Ventspils, when he lied to her about her father, his left thumb had gone to the cuff of his right sleeve, and she had learned to watch for it. His hands lay on the table in front of him, open and quite still. They did not move once.

“There is one more thing,” he said. “On the ship, on the second morning, you were photographing the file. When I lifted the papers, the camera slid out of them. I saw it fall. I said nothing.”

Sanchez stared at him. For eleven days she had believed he had not seen it. She had built half her courage on that belief.

“Why?”

“Because I had already chosen. I did not know it yet, but I had. And at Gdynia, in the hold, I told you what the statue was so that you would know what you had.” He took a pause. “I am not telling you this so that you will think better of me. You asked for everything. That is everything.”

“And now?”

“When your father is home, I will give your Maxwell the route. All of it. The maps, the ship, the warehouse, the names in Ayr.”

“He is not my Maxwell.”

“No. But he is the one who can use it.”

She got up and went to the window. The yard below was dark, and the BMW stood by the fence with the six pistols still lying in the boot under a blanket, where Stephen had put them. She thought of her father on the pavement with the bakery bag, and of the man in the back seat who had watched and done nothing, and of the same man with a pistol at her temple that afternoon, saying he would always love her. She had come to this room ready to hate him. She found that she could not do it completely, and she did not know whether that was a weakness in her or the truth about him.

“I do not know if I can forgive you,” she said at last.

“I have not asked you to.” He stood up. “Tomorrow, when your father crosses, go with him. There will be nothing here to keep you. Your room is across the landing. You should sleep.”

Sanchez did not go back to her room. She sat down again at the table across from him, and after a while he sat down too, and they talked on into the night about nothing that was resolved, until the lamp was the only light left in the street. At midnight she went out onto the landing and rang her brother, and Stephen apologised three times before she could tell him that it was not him she was angry with. In the morning Benedikt would drive through the gate at Smolensk alone, while she waited for him in the birches, and she could not stop thinking that Mr Kozlov, who was “not here until late”, had had the whole night to decide what to do with them.

## Chapter 58
### 9:00 PM, Saturday 3rd May 2014, Smolensk, Russia

While Sanchez waited for dark in the guesthouse at Rudnya, Gennady Kozlov was waiting for a telephone call, and he was displeased. At lunchtime Evan Slater had listened to him for two minutes, said he would speak to Voss himself, and rung off. Since then the telephone on Kozlov’s desk had stayed silent, and he was not a man who liked to wait on another man’s pleasure. Outside his office window the forest had gone dark at its edges. Across the yard a single light burned in the room above the workshop, where the Scot slept.

The telephone rang at nine. Kozlov let it ring three times before he answered.

“I’ve spoken to Voss,” Slater said. There was no greeting. There never was. “He says he was there when the Scot was taken, and the man is a risk to us while he lives. He wants to let him go and follow him. If he goes to the police, Voss finishes it. If he doesn’t, we leave him be.”

“And you believe him?”

“I’ve believed him for four years.” Slater’s voice was clipped and tired. “He has never given me a reason not to.”

Kozlov turned his chair to the window. The light above the workshop was still on. For seven months he had kept the Scot alive, and not out of kindness. The copy the man had boasted of had never been found. Besides, a living Daniel was a thing Kozlov owned. If TransPacific ever cheated him, the Scot could be put on a road to Minsk with a British embassy at the end of it, and Slater knew it. It was the only hold he had on the man in Troon, and he did not like to be asked to give it up.

“There is one more thing,” Kozlov said. “Voss did not come alone. Anatoly tells me there was a girl with him yesterday, and again this morning. He calls her his assistant. She sat in the room and did not understand one word of Russian.”

The line was silent for so long that Kozlov thought it had dropped.

“What girl?”

“Young. Dark hair. Not Russian. Anatoly took her for his woman.” Kozlov paused. “She watched Anatoly’s face the whole time, he says, as if she was waiting for something.”

“He has a temp on the ship,” Slater said slowly. “From the Copenhagen office. A temp doesn’t go to Smolensk.” He took a breath, and when he spoke again his voice had changed. “The thief at Helsingør was a girl, Gennady. He told me she was dealt with.”

Kozlov said nothing. He had learned long ago that silence made other men say more than they meant.

“Let him take the Scot,” Slater said at last. “Follow them. If he hands him to anyone, the police, the girl, anyone, finish it on the road. Both of them. I want no more loose ends from that man.”

It was not a request. Kozlov knew it, and he knew what it cost him. Everything he sold in Moscow came to him down TransPacific’s route, and the Mỹ Sơn lots alone were worth more than the compound and everyone in it. He could not refuse TransPacific.

“It will be done,” he said, and the line went dead.

He called for two of his men. They came in from the yard smelling of tobacco and stood before his desk while he spoke to them in Russian, in short sentences, as he always did. They would take the silver Toyota. They would stay far back, for Voss was no fool. If Voss drove into Belarus, or stopped for anyone on the road, they knew what to do. They asked no questions, and he did not expect them to.

When they had gone he sat on in the dark for a while. Across the yard the light above the workshop went out. In the yard below, one of the men was already filling the tank of a silver Toyota Camry with 67 on its plates.

* * *

*The compound, 8:00 AM, Sunday 4th May*

Benedikt drove the last three miles to the compound. It was a grey morning. It had rained in the night, and the mist still lay low among the birches on either side of the road. This time he had decided to go in alone. Unlike last time, Sanchez was reasonable and understood the rationale behind it. The guard at the gate had looked into the boot on both visits, so he had left her at a lay-by three miles back with the six police pistols from the Liozna road rolled in a blanket at her feet, and the Makarov in her bag. The way things were turning, he was unsure what to expect, and he wanted nothing in the car that a guard could find.

The guard lifted the boot lid, looked at the spare wheel and nothing else, and waved him through.

Anatoly was at his deceitful best. He met Benedikt on the steps with the same verve as the day before, his grin wide and fixed, his blue eyes missing nothing. He offered coffee, and Benedikt declined it with a smile of his own.

“Mr Kozlov saw him last night,” Anatoly said as they walked in. “He wishes you a pleasant journey.”

“Please thank him for me.”

“And your man? The one who will follow the Scot?”

“He is waiting on the road,” Benedikt said. It was not a lie, and he said it without any hesitation.

Anatoly nodded as though this was the most natural arrangement in the world. The office was the same as before, bare and overheated, with a portrait of the President on the wall and a map of the oblast beside it. Anatoly spoke to a young man at the door in Russian and sat down behind the desk. Benedikt did not sit. He stood by the window with his hands in his coat pockets and watched the yard.

He had seen the man once already, on Friday, from the far end of a long workshop, bent over a crate with a hammer in his hand. Anatoly had been showing him the place and had not so much as glanced in that direction. Benedikt had not stopped walking. He had looked for perhaps two seconds, and it had been enough. The face in Sanchez’s photograph, the face in the Range Rover, the face in the café kitchen on the Vitebsk ring road were all the same face, older now, and it had lifted at the sound of footsteps and looked straight at him.

The door opened, and Daniel was brought to the room.

He looked frail and confused. He was thinner than Benedikt remembered, even from Friday, and his face was covered with a white beard. The quilted jacket he wore had been made for a bigger man, and his trousers were gathered at the waist with a belt that had too many holes punched in it. In one hand he carried a plastic bag, and Benedikt understood that it held everything he owned. His hands were scarred across the knuckles from the crates. Nobody had told him why he was there.

Then he saw Benedikt, and he stopped in the doorway.

It lasted no more than a second. His eyes went to Benedikt’s face and stayed there, and his hand closed on the plastic bag until the knuckles went white. Benedikt gave nothing away. It was the face he wore at the Helsingør table, and he had not needed it in a long time. He knew that Anatoly was watching them both.

“Daniel,” Anatoly said in his careful English, “this gentleman will take you away from here. You are going on a journey.” He came round the desk and, to Benedikt’s surprise, took the Scot’s hand and held it a moment longer than business required. “Good luck to you.”

Daniel looked at him and said nothing. Anatoly’s grin faltered for the first time, and then it was back.

They walked out together into the yard, Daniel a pace ahead. The young man from the door came with them as far as the steps and stopped there. The guard at the gate had come out of his hut. Two men were smoking by the workshop and had stopped talking. Benedikt felt every pair of eyes in that yard on his back, and he kept his pace even.

Halfway to the car Daniel spoke without turning his head. His voice was low, Scottish and quite steady.

“You were in the back seat. And at the café.”

Benedikt did not answer. He could not, with Anatoly on the steps behind him. He walked round the man, opened the rear door of the car and held it.

“Please,” he said.

For a while Daniel did not move. He looked at Benedikt across the open door, and Benedikt made himself hold that look. Then the Scot bent and got into the back seat with his plastic bag on his knees, and Benedikt closed the door on him.

He had his share of doubts, for it had gone too smoothly to appear real. Anatoly raised a hand from the steps. Benedikt raised his in return, got in and started the engine. At the gate the guard lifted the barrier without looking in the boot, and the forest closed round the road behind them.

Neither of them spoke for the first mile. In the mirror Benedikt could see the Scot sitting very upright, his eyes on the back of Benedikt’s head. He remembered that voice. Four years ago, in the Range Rover, it had said *if I don’t come home, the pictures go to the police and the papers*, and it had not shaken then either.

“Your daughter is waiting for you,” Benedikt said. “Three miles down this road.”

The Scot said nothing for a long time.

“Sanchez?” he said at last.

“Yes.”

“If this is some trick of yours –”

“It is not a trick.”

“Then why are you doing it?”

Benedikt kept his eyes on the road. There was an answer, and it was a long one, and it belonged to her.

“She will tell you,” he said.

He was watchful, and for now all that mattered was Daniel’s safety. He looked in the mirror again. Behind them the road ran back through the birches to the compound, grey and wet and empty, and he could see nothing following them out of the forest. For a whole mile he watched that empty road, and he let himself believe it.

## Chapter 59
### 8:40 AM, Sunday 4th May 2014, the Rudnya road, Russia

Sanchez had been standing at the edge of the lay-by for an hour, and she had looked at her watch more times than she could count. The birches dripped around her. The mist had begun to lift from the road but still lay in the hollows of the fields, white and low, and the pistols lay rolled in their blanket at her feet where Benedikt had left them. Twice a car had passed. Each time she had stepped back among the trees, and each time it had been nobody.

At midnight, on the landing of the guesthouse at Rudnya, she had rung Stephen. She had refused to speak to Maxwell, but she had given Stephen her terms for him. She wanted two marked police cars at the blue sign from eight o’clock, Belarus time, on the Belarusian side and in plain view, and she wanted Major Zhuk there himself. Nobody was to cross towards them, and nobody was to touch Benedikt so long as he stayed in Russia. Ten minutes later Stephen had rung back with Maxwell’s answer, which was yes. She had heard Maxwell’s word before. She was trusting it once more because there was nobody else to trust.

Sanchez spotted the car from a distance. She was numb, as if her feet were frozen to the ground, and despite the strong urge she failed to run towards the car to check her father’s presence.

Benedikt’s car came to a complete halt, and Sanchez saw her father behind the glass. He looked wearily thin. The clothes he was wearing were larger than his frame. His face was covered with a white beard and his hair appeared dishevelled. His complexion was a shade below pale. One thing that hadn’t changed was his smile, which had been glued to her memories all these years since he disappeared. This time she leapt forward with all her might and hugged him tightly as he climbed out. They were both crying. There were no words. All she could see through her own tears was his face, wet and smiling.

“Dad, you are free. We will all live together like we used to.” The words found their way out in a trembling voice.

Her father could manage only a few words through his choking voice. “Thank you. I never thought I would ever see you again.”

He held her away from him to look at her, the way he used to when she came home from school. Then his eyes went past her to Benedikt, who had lifted the blanket and its load into the boot and was closing it without a sound.

“San, that man –”

“I know, Dad. He told me.”

He looked at her for a long time. Whatever he saw in her face, he did not argue with it. He got back into the car, and she got in beside him and held his hand, and Benedikt drove on without a word. She gave Benedikt the Makarov over the back of the seat, and he laid it on the seat beside him.

They passed through Rudnya, where the shutters of the guesthouse were still closed and a woman in a headscarf was sweeping the steps of the church. Beyond the town the road ran straight and empty through the birches towards Belarus. Her father looked out at the mist in the fields.

“Like a haar at home,” he said.

For a while he said nothing more, and she did not press him. She was content to feel his hand in hers, thin and rough and warm. Then, without turning from the window, he began to speak.

“I’ll tell you now, San, because I’ve only ever told one man, and he sold me.” He took a deep breath. “It was a Tuesday. The second of February.”

“I know,” she said. “It’s in your diary. Sealed pkg, E.S. office.”

He turned and looked at her. “You read my diary.”

“I read everything, Dad.”

Something that was almost a laugh came out of him. “Aye. You would.” He was serious again. “John Patterson gave me a crate at the depot. Wrapped and sealed, with a waybill signed by Evan Slater himself. Garden statuary, reproduction, for Bay 7 in the bond at Prestwick. They used me because I wasn’t on their books. No record, you see. John didn’t like it. He wouldn’t look at me when he handed it over.”

“He helped me in the end,” she said. “He’s still frightened.”

“So he should be.” There was no anger in it. “On the A77 I took the roundabout at Monkton too fast, and the crate went over in the back. When I pulled in to put it right, the wrapping had split down one side.” He was quiet for a moment. “There was a face in there, San. Gold. A woman dancing, with a crown on her head like a wee temple. She wasn’t a reproduction of anything. You could see a thousand years on her.”

“I photographed her sister in Gdynia,” Sanchez said. “Last week. In the hold of the DAZZLE.”

“The DAZZLE,” he said, and for a moment he was somewhere else. In the mirror she saw Benedikt’s eyes go to her father’s face and back to the road.

“At Bay 7 there were a dozen more crates, all marked the same. I had the wee camera in my jacket, the one your mother gave me for Christmas. I took the lady, and I took the crates. I gave the clerk the carbon and I kept the top copy of the waybill.” He paused. “I told myself I didn’t know why. I did know. It had his name on it.”

“And then you went to the police.”

“On the Thursday I went to Iain MacCallum. I’d known him twenty years. I showed him the pictures on the camera, and he wrote it all down in his own hand at the desk in Girvan. Told me to bring him prints.” Her father’s voice did not change, but his hand tightened on hers. “Eight days later he told me Customs had looked. They were reproductions, and I was to leave it. He wouldn’t look at me either. That was when I knew.”

“He stopped looking at us too,” Sanchez said. “He stopped answering the phone. He retired last year.”

“Did he,” her father said.

The road ran on through the birches. A lorry passed going the other way and the car rocked in its wake.

“That night I took the card out of the camera, folded up the waybill and put them both in my dad’s old tobacco tin. The Golden Virginia one.” For the first time he smiled at her properly. “Under your window, San. The board I never fixed.”

She stared at him. For a moment she was not in a car in Russia at all. She was sixteen, in her nightdress, with her elbows on the sill and her sleeve over her hand, and the board that had rocked under her all winter was firm for once. She remembered thinking that he had fixed it at last.

“It didn’t move,” she said. “That morning. It didn’t move under my elbows.”

“I wedged it tight on the Friday night. I was going to take the tin to the Customs in Glasgow on the Monday.” He looked out at the fields. “I never got to Monday.”

She did not ask about the Sunday. He told her anyway, in a few plain sentences. There was a black car by the bakery with its engine running. He had got ten yards from the bakery door with the bag in his hand when there were men on either side of him, and one of them spoke Russian.

“I told them if I didn’t come home, the pictures would go to the police and the papers. It was the only card I had, and it held.” He shook his head slowly. “That’s the only reason I’m sitting here.”

“And you never told them where.”

“I never told them, San. Four years. Not on the ship, not in Novka, not in that place in the forest. They asked often enough.” He looked down at their hands. “And I never told you. If I’d told your mother, she’d have gone straight to MacCallum. And a letter would have brought them to the door and sent them looking under your window. So I kept my mouth shut and hoped you’d all forgive me for it.”

Sanchez could not speak. She held his hand in both of hers. All those years she had searched for what he knew, and it had been under her elbows every morning she stood at that window.

“I have his signature too, Dad,” she said at last. “On this year’s insurance schedule. Garden statuary, reproduction, authorised E. Slater. And his voice on a recording, approving the Mỹ Sơn lots.”

Her father thought about it. “Four years apart,” he said. “The same crates.”

“The same case.”

It was then that she noticed Benedikt. He had not spoken since the lay-by, but his eyes had gone to the mirror more and more often, and the car had slowed a little. She turned in her seat. Far back along the straight, where the road came out of a dip, there was a car. It was silver, and it was keeping its distance.

“How long?” she asked.

“Since the gate, I think. I saw it first at Rudnya.” Benedikt’s voice was calm. “A Toyota. Smolensk plates.” He glanced at her in the mirror. “Do not turn round again. Please.”

She turned back. Her father was looking at her, and then at the back of Benedikt’s head.

“They did nothing in the town,” Benedikt said. “There were too many people. But there is nothing between here and the sign except trees.”

“And at the sign?”

“At the sign there is nothing at all.” He took the Makarov from the seat beside him, pushed the safety down and laid it in his lap. “No barrier and no guard. Nobody official will stop anyone on this road, from either side. If they mean to finish it, it will be on the last stretch.”

Sanchez did not tell him about the police cars. If Maxwell broke his word a second time, she wanted Benedikt ready for it. She put her arm through her father’s. He did not ask who they were. He had lived among them long enough.

The silver car came closer. She did not turn round, but she could see it in the mirror above Benedikt’s head, growing, holding back, growing again. Benedikt kept his speed steady. The birches ran past on both sides, and the road ahead ran straight and grey to a blue sign standing on its own at the verge, with the same birches on either side of it.

Then the silver car put on speed. Sanchez was frightened.

Benedikt’s hand went to the Makarov, and she felt the car begin to slow.

“Don’t stop,” she said. “Drive on to the sign, Benedikt, at the same speed. Please.”

He looked at her in the mirror, and he did as she asked. Her father’s arm tightened under hers. The silver car filled the mirror now, and she could see two men in the front, and the one beside the driver was leaning forward.

Beyond the blue sign, two white cars were parked on the verges, one on either side of the road. They had blue lights on their roofs, turning slowly in the grey morning, and a word she could not read, МІЛІЦЫЯ, in blue letters down their sides. A heavy man in a grey coat stood in the road between them with his hands in his pockets, and she supposed he was Major Zhuk. Beside him four officers in uniform stood quite still, and they were watching the road.

The silver car slowed.

It slowed until it was barely moving, a hundred yards behind them, and then it stopped in the middle of the road. For a long minute nobody moved, not the Toyota, not the men in the road, not Benedikt, who had brought the car to a halt short of the sign with the engine running. Sanchez watched in the mirror with her heart in her mouth.

Then the Toyota turned. It turned slowly across the road, in three movements, as if the driver wanted everyone to see that he was in no hurry, and it drove back the way it had come until the birches hid it.

Benedikt pushed the safety up and put the Makarov down. Sanchez looked at the white cars and the heavy man and the turning lights. For once, Maxwell had done exactly what she had asked of him.

Beyond the police cars the road ran on for a few hundred yards and bent away into the trees. At the far end, where it bent, a man had stepped out into the road and was waving both arms above his head.

Her father leaned forward between the seats. “Is that Stephen?” he said, and she could not answer him.

## Chapter 60
### 9:30 AM, Sunday 4th May 2014, the border near Liozna, Russia

Sanchez’s phone rang before anyone in the car had moved. She knew who it was without looking. At the far end of the road the small figure had stopped waving and was holding something to its ear.

“Is he there?” Stephen’s voice was high and breathless. “Sanchez, is he really there?”

She could not trust herself to answer. She put the phone on speaker and held it out to her father.

He took it in both hands as though it might break. “Stephen.”

There was a silence on the line, and then a sound she had never heard her brother make, not even in the week the police gave up.

“Dad?”

“It’s me, son.” Her father’s voice was steady, but his hands were not. “Your mother?”

“She’s at home. She doesn’t know anything. I didn’t want to tell her till I’d seen you.” Stephen was laughing and crying at once. “Hold on, Dad. Hold on. I’ll get her.”

The line clicked and went quiet, and then it was ringing, far away, in a house in the Carrick hills where it was half past six on a Sunday morning. Sanchez knew exactly how it would sound. The phone stood on the table in the hall at the foot of the stairs. It rang five times, six, and then someone picked it up.

“Stephen?” Her mother’s voice was still full of sleep. “What is it? Is it Sanchez? Has something happened?”

Her father closed his eyes. “Bel,” he said.

The line was silent. It was silent for so long that Sanchez thought her mother had put the phone down, or fainted, or not heard. Then her mother said his name. She said it once, and her voice broke in the middle of it, and after that there were no more words from Girbin for a while, only the sound of a woman weeping in a hall at the foot of the stairs.

Sanchez turned her face to the side window so that her father would not see it. She had pictured that kitchen every night for four years. She had pictured the door opening and her father coming in out of the cold, and her mother turning from the stove, and herself at the table. She would not be at the table. She understood that now, listening to her mother’s voice break, and she understood what it would cost her.

“Where are you?” her mother managed at last. “Daniel, where are you?”

“A long way off, Bel. But I’m coming home. Stephen’s bringing me.” He leaned forward, and his voice changed, and it was the voice she remembered from the Sunday mornings, the one that made plans. “Bel, listen to me now. I need you to do something for me. Go up to San’s room. The window. The board under the sill lifts at the left end.”

“The board?”

“Go on, love. I’ll wait.”

They heard her go up the stairs, the creak of the fourth step, a door. Sanchez knew every sound of it. Then there was a scraping, and a small hard knock of wood, and her mother’s breathing close to the phone.

“There is a tin,” Isabel said. “Your father’s tin. The tobacco tin.”

“Open it.”

“There is a little card, like for a camera. And a paper, folded.” Paper rustled. “It says Bay 7. It says garden statuary. And at the bottom it is signed.” She read it out slowly, in her careful voice with the Spanish still under it. “E. Slater.”

“That’s the one,” her father said. “Put it back in the tin, Bel, and keep it by you. Don’t let anyone have it but Stephen, or a policeman he brings to the door. Not MacCallum. Nobody from Girvan.”

“Daniel, what is this? What have you done?”

“Nothing I’m ashamed of. I’ll tell you all of it. I’ve a lot to tell you.”

There was a pause. Then her mother said, in a different voice, “Stephen? Where is Sanchez? Is she –”

Sanchez took a moment before she could speak. “I’m here, Mum.”

“*Hija*.” Her mother was crying again. “Come home, all of you. Come home.”

Sanchez could not answer her. Her father looked at her, and then he took the phone off speaker and held it to his ear and said something low that she did not hear, and after a while he said goodbye and gave the phone back to her. Stephen was on the line again.

“Maxwell says the embassy in Minsk will have papers for Dad by tomorrow night,” Stephen said. “He’s talked to them already. He told his boss everything yesterday. It’s all official now. Sanchez, come on. Bring him.”

“I’m sorry, Stephen,” she said.

“What for? Come on, the pair of you.”

She did not answer him. She rang off and got out of the car. Her father got out on the other side, with his plastic bag, and stood in the road looking towards the blue sign and the white cars and the small figure beyond them. Benedikt had got out too. He stayed by the driver’s door and said nothing, and he did not come round the car.

“You should go now,” he said at last. It was the first thing he had said since the silver car turned back.

Sanchez did not answer him. She took her father’s hands. “Dad, Stephen is waiting for you. Maxwell has arranged all the necessary protection. Soon TransPacific will be exposed and we will all be together.”

She took a deep breath and continued, “You need to walk across the border alone. I can’t come now. I need to take care of a few things.”

Her father looked worried. He looked over the roof of the car at Benedikt for a long time. Benedikt did not look away. Neither of them said anything, and Sanchez did not know what passed between them in that look. Then her father gave him a small nod, once, the way he used to nod to a man who had done a hard job properly, and turned back to her.

“San,” her father said. “I said I’d take you to all those places.”

“I went on my own, Dad.”

He smiled, the smile she had carried for four years, and put his hand on her cheek. “I’m sure you have your reasons. Don’t make me wait for long this time.”

Time halted and they hugged for a long while. They parted with reluctance, and slowly her father walked away down the middle of the road. He walked past the blue sign without looking at it. The officers stood aside for him, and the heavy man in the grey coat nodded to him. At the far end Stephen had begun to run. She watched the two of them meet, small at the end of the long straight, and hold on to each other, and then they turned together and went round the bend in the road, and the trees hid them.

Sanchez stood in the road and looked at the bend where they had gone. Only then did she see a man in a dark windcheater standing apart beside the second police car with his hood up. It was Maxwell. He did not come forward, and she did not wave to him.

Behind her she heard the boot open. Benedikt walked past her with the blanket in his arms. He went up to the blue sign and knelt on the verge beside it, unrolled the blanket, and one by one he laid six pistols in the wet grass, side by side. Then he stood up, lifted a hand to the heavy man in the grey coat and walked back. The man in the grey coat lifted a hand in return, and did not move from where he stood.

Her phone buzzed in her hand. It was a text from a number she did not know.

*Daniel across. Police Scotland at Slater’s door in Troon, 06:50. – M.*

She read it twice. In Troon it was twenty to seven. In ten minutes Evan Slater would open his front door to the police.

She put the phone in her pocket, walked back to the car and got into the back seat, where her father had sat. The seat was still warm.

Benedikt was halfway into the driver’s seat when he saw her. He froze where he was, as if lightning had struck him, and kept staring at her with astonishment.

“After what you did on that road, the Russians will not spare you,” she said. “Maxwell has everything he needs to finish TransPacific. Let us get out of this place before the Russians come looking for us.”

“Us?” Benedikt was still in a shocked state.

“Yes, us.”

He looked at her without a word. Then he got in and closed the door. His hands were quite still on the wheel.

“Yes, and for a lifetime,” he said.

Benedikt turned the car round on the empty road and drove back the way they had come. The rear window had misted over while they sat there, and the road behind them was a grey blur. Sanchez stretched her sleeve over her hand and wiped it clear. The blue sign was already small. Beyond it the road ran on between the birches and bent away out of sight, and the bend was empty.
